**Question type:** cofactor

<table>
<thead><tr><th>Original question</th><th>Skill-informed rewrite</th></tr></thead>
<tbody><tr>
<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> Which metal ion is the cofactor associated with this protein&#x27;s ATP-dependent adenylyl-transferase activity?<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> Mg2+<br><br><strong>B.</strong> Mn2+<br><br><strong>C.</strong> Zn2+<br><br><strong>D.</strong> Fe2+<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>1. reasoning: Analyzing the protein sequence for cofactor identification:<br><br>- The protein sequence (~200 amino acids) shows characteristics of an adenylyltransferase, including nucleotide-binding motifs and the &quot;HHG&quot; region which can participate in metal coordination.<br>- The question specifies &quot;ATP-dependent adenylyl-transferase activity,&quot; meaning this enzyme transfers an adenylyl (AMP) group from ATP to a substrate.<br>- For ATP-dependent enzymes, the metal ion must coordinate and stabilize the negatively charged phosphate groups of ATP.<br>- Mg²⁺ is the most common and physiologically relevant divalent cation for ATP coordination in enzymatic reactions. It neutralizes the negative charges on ATP&#x27;s phosphate groups, stabilizes the transition state, and is present at appropriate physiological concentrations.<br>- While Mn²⁺ can substitute in some enzymes, Mg²⁺ is the canonical cofactor for the vast majority of ATP-dependent adenylyltransferases.<br>- Zn²⁺ is typically found in structural or catalytic roles in other enzyme classes (e.g., zinc metalloproteases), not typically in ATP-binding sites.<br>- Fe²⁺ is more commonly associated with redox reactions and oxygen transport proteins.<br>- The sequence characteristics and the general mechanism of adenylyltransferases strongly support Mg²⁺ as the cofactor.<br><br>2. answer: A</td>
